### Foreign relations

Main article: [Foreign relations of South Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_South_Korea)



Former [Secretary-General of the United Nations](162415.md) (2007–2016), [Ban Ki-moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ban_Ki-moon)

South Korea has been a member of the United Nations since 1991, when it became a member state at the same time as North Korea. South Korea has developed links with the [Association of Southeast Asian Nations](106539.md) as both a member of *ASEAN Plus three*, a body of observers, and the [East Asia Summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Asia_Summit) (EAS).

A [close relationship](5748222.md) with the United States began directly after World War II, when the United States temporarily administered South Korea. Upon the onset of the Korean War in 1950, U.S. forces were sent to defend against an invasion from North Korea of the South and subsequently fought [as the largest contributor of UN troops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_in_the_Korean_War). Following the Armistice, South Korea and the U.S. agreed to a "Mutual Defense Treaty", under which an attack on either party in the [Pacific area](797909.md) would summon a response from both.[^cite_note-172] The two nations also share a close economic relationship,[^cite_note-MIT-2018-173]

Historically, Korea had close relations with the dynasties in China, and some Korean kingdoms were members of the [Imperial Chinese tributary system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tributary_system_of_China). The Korean kingdoms also ruled over some Chinese kingdoms, including the Khitan people and the Manchurians, before the Qing dynasty, and received tributes from them.[^cite_note-174] Though at opposing sides during the Cold War, South Korean–Chinese relations have improved steadily since 1992.[^cite_note-Asia_Times-2004-175] China has become South Korea's largest trading partner by far.[^cite_note-MIT-2018-173]

Korea and Japan have had ties since ancient times but also significant cultural exchange, with Korea acting as the gateway between East Asia and Japan. Longstanding issues such as historical disputes over [Japan's 35-year colonization of Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korea_under_Japanese_rule) in the 20th century and [Japanese war crimes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_war_crimes) against Korean civilians, the territorial disputes over the [Liancourt Rocks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liancourt_Rocks), known in South Korea as "Dokdo" and in Japan as "Takeshima",[^cite_note-176] and visits by Japanese politicians to the [Yasukuni Shrine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yasukuni_Shrine) continue to trouble Korean-Japanese relations. Ties between the two countries have improved significantly in the 2020s, and polls show that public perceptions of each other have become much more positive, especially among younger generations.[^cite_note-177] Japan is today South Korea's third largest trading partner.[^cite_note-MIT-2018-173]

#### North Korea

Main article: [North Korea–South Korea relations](5882788.md)



The [Joint Security Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_Security_Area)

South Korea claims complete sovereignty over the entire peninsula and outlying islands.[^cite_note-178] North and South Korea are still technically at war (having never signed a peace treaty after the Korean War) and share the world's most heavily fortified border.[^cite_note-border-179] Despite mutual animosity, reconciliation efforts have continued since the initial separation between North and South Korea.[^cite_note-180] During the late 1990s and 2000s, South Korea pursued the [Sunshine Policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunshine_Policy), promoting improved ties with North Korea.[^cite_note-Reuters-2007-181]

Despite the Sunshine Policy and efforts at reconciliation, the progress was complicated by [North Korean missile tests](5831012.md) in [1993](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1993_North_Korean_missile_test), [1998](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1998_North_Korean_missile_test), [2006](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_North_Korean_nuclear_test), [2009](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009_North_Korean_nuclear_test), and [2013](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_North_Korean_missile_tests). By early 2009, relationships between North and South Korea were very tense; North Korea had been reported to have deployed missiles,[^cite_note-182] ended its former agreements with South Korea,[^cite_note-183] and threatened South Korea and the United States not to interfere with a satellite launch it had planned.[^cite_note-184] Relations improved in the late 2010s but deteriorated again in the 2020s. In 2024, in response to increasing tensions, North Korea abandoned peaceful reunification plans and labeled South Korea as the most hostile country to North Korea.[^cite_note-185][^cite_note-186]

### Military

Main article: [Republic of Korea Armed Forces](27027.md)



[ROKN](2284275.md) [*Sejong the Great*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ROKS_Sejong_the_Great), a [*Sejong the Great*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sejong_the_Great-class_destroyer)-class [guided-missile destroyer](232005.md) built by [Hyundai Heavy Industries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyundai_Heavy_Industries)

Unresolved tension with North Korea has prompted South Korea to allocate 2.6% of its GDP and 13.2% of all government spending to its military (government share of GDP: 14.967%), while maintaining compulsory conscription for men.[^cite_note-187] Consequently, the ROK Armed Forces is one of the largest and most powerful standing armed forces in the world with a reported personnel strength of 3,600,000 in 2022 (500,000 active and 3,100,000 reserve).[^cite_note-188]

The South Korean military consists of the [Army](1515574.md) (ROKA), the [Navy](2284275.md) (ROKN), the [Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Korea_Air_Force) (ROKAF), and the [Marine Corps](2052682.md) (ROKMC), and reserve forces. Many of these forces are concentrated near the Korean Demilitarized Zone. All South Korean males are constitutionally required to serve in the military, typically for 18 months.[^cite_note-189] In addition, the [Korean Augmentation to the United States Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_Augmentation_to_the_United_States_Army) is a branch of the Republic of Korea Army that consists of Korean enlisted personnel who are augmented to the Eighth United States Army. In 2010, South Korea spent [₩](298884.md)1.68 trillion in a cost-sharing agreement with the U.S. to provide budgetary support to the U.S. forces in Korea, on top of the ₩29.6 trillion budget for its own military.

From time to time, South Korea has sent its troops overseas to assist American forces. It has participated in most major conflicts that the United States has been involved in over the past 50 years. South Korea dispatched 325,517 troops to fight in the [Vietnam War](32611.md), with a peak strength of 50,000.[^cite_note-190] In 2004, South Korea sent 3,300 troops of the [Zaytun Division](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zaytun_Division) to help rebuilding in northern [Iraq](7515928.md), and was the third largest contributor in the [coalition forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multi-National_Force_%E2%80%93_Iraq) after the U.S. and Britain.[^cite_note-191] Beginning in 2001, South Korea had deployed 24,000 troops in the Middle East region to support the [war on terror](13425800.md).

The right to [conscientious objection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conscientious_objection) was not recognized in South Korea until recently. Over 400 men were typically imprisoned at any given time for refusing military service for political or religious reasons in the years before the right to conscientious objection was established.[^cite_note-192] On June 28, 2018, the South Korean Constitutional Court ruled the Military Service Act unconstitutional and ordered the government to accommodate civilian forms of military service for conscientious objectors.[^cite_note-193] On November 1, 2018, the South Korean Supreme Court legalized conscientious objection as a basis for rejecting compulsory military service.[^cite_note-194]

#### United States contingent

There is a substantial [United States military presence in South Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_United_States_Army_installations_in_South_Korea). There are approximately 28,500 U.S. military personnel stationed in South Korea,[^cite_note-195] most of them serving one-year unaccompanied tours. The U.S. troops, which are primarily ground and air units, are assigned to [United States Forces Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Forces_Korea) and mainly assigned to the [Eighth Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eighth_United_States_Army), [Seventh Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seventh_Air_Force), and [Naval Forces Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Naval_Forces_Korea). They are stationed in installations at [Osan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osan), [Kunsan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kunsan), Yongsan, [Dongducheon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dongducheon), Sungbuk, [Camp Humphreys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camp_Humphreys), and [Daegu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daegu), as well as at [Camp Bonifas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camp_Bonifas) in the DMZ [Joint Security Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_Security_Area).

A fully functioning [UN Command](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Command) is at the top of the [chain of command](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chain_of_command) of all forces in South Korea, including the U.S. forces and the entire South Korean military – if a sudden escalation of war between North and South Korea were to occur, the United States would assume control of the South Korean armed forces in all military and paramilitary moves. There has been a long-term agreement between the United States and South Korea that South Korea should eventually assume the lead for its own defense. This transition to a South Korean command has been slow and often postponed, although it is currently scheduled to occur in the 2020s.[^cite_note-196]

## Economy

Main article: [Economy of South Korea](27024.md)

See also: [List of largest companies of South Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_largest_companies_of_South_Korea)



The [Bank of Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bank_of_Korea), the central bank of South Korea and issuer of the [South Korean won](298884.md)



The Samsung headquarters in [Samsung Town](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samsung_Town), located in [Seocho District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seocho_District), Seoul



The [Lotte World Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lotte_World_Tower) in [Songpa District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Songpa_District), Seoul, is the [tallest building in South Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_tallest_buildings_in_South_Korea) and the [6th tallest in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_tallest_buildings).

South Korea's [mixed economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mixed_economy)[^cite_note-197][^cite_note-198][^cite_note-199] is the [14th-largest by nominal GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(nominal\)) and the 14th-largest GDP by purchasing power parity in the world,[^cite_note-International_Monetary_Fund-2023-200] identifying it as one of the [G20](2206608.md) major economies. It is a [developed country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Developed_country) with a [high-income economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Bank_high-income_economy) and is the most industrialized member country of the OECD. South Korean brands such as [LG Electronics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LG_Electronics) and [Samsung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samsung) are internationally famous and have garnered South Korea's reputation for its high-quality electronics and other manufactured goods.[^cite_note-201] South Korea became a member of the OECD in 1996.[^cite_note-202]

Its massive investment in education has taken the country from mass illiteracy to a major international technological powerhouse. The country's national economy benefits from a highly skilled workforce and is among the most educated countries in the world, with one of the highest percentages of its citizens holding a tertiary education degree.[^cite_note-OECD-203] South Korea's economy was one of the world's fastest-growing from the early 1960s to the late 1990s, and was still one of the fastest-growing developed countries in the 2000s, along with [Hong Kong](13404.md), [Singapore](27318.md) and [Taiwan](25734.md), the other three [Asian Tigers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Four_Asian_Tigers).[^cite_note-204] It recorded the [fastest rise in average GDP per capita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(real\)_per_capita_growth_rate) in the world between 1980 and 1990.[^cite_note-205] South Koreans refer to this growth as the [Miracle on the Han River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miracle_on_the_Han_River).[^cite_note-206] The South Korean economy is heavily dependent on international trade, and in 2014, South Korea was the [fifth-largest exporter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_exports) and [seventh-largest importer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_imports) in the world. In addition, the country has one of the world's [largest foreign-exchange reserves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_foreign-exchange_reserves).[^cite_note-207] South Korea's economy relies significantly on semiconductor and other AI-related gear exports, which account for around 40% of its total exports. The ratio of South Korea's exports to GDP is 46%. Consumption, by contrast, plays a relatively small role, accounting for 40% of South Korea's GDP.[^cite_note-the_economist-2026-208]

Despite the economy's high growth potential and apparent structural stability, the country suffers damage to its credit rating in the stock market because of the belligerence of North Korea in times of deep military crises, which has an adverse effect on its financial markets.[^cite_note-kcredit-209][^cite_note-210] Additionally, economic growth is increasingly concentrated in a small number of tech-related companies, with smaller businesses that account for 60% of employment seeing slower growth, while the country faces economic competition from China.[^cite_note-the_economist-2026-208] The [International Monetary Fund](15251.md) compliments the resilience of the economy against various economic crises, citing low state debt and high fiscal reserves that can quickly be mobilized to address financial emergencies.[^cite_note-211] Although it was severely harmed by the [1997 Asian financial crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1997_Asian_financial_crisis), the country managed a rapid recovery and subsequently tripled its GDP.[^cite_note-212] Furthermore, South Korea was one of the few developed countries that was able to avoid a recession during the [2008 financial crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_financial_crisis).[^cite_note-213]

### Transportation

Main article: [Transport in South Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transport_in_South_Korea)



[Incheon International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Incheon_International_Airport)'s [Maglev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Incheon_Airport_Maglev) station

South Korea has a technologically advanced transport network consisting of high-speed railways, highways, bus routes, ferry services, and air routes that crisscross the country. [Korea Expressway Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korea_Expressway_Corporation) operates the toll highways and service amenities en route. [Korail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korail) provides train services to all major South Korean cities. Two rail lines, the [Gyeongui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gyeongui_Line) and [Donghae Bukbu Lines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donghae_Bukbu_Line), to North Korea are being reconnected. The Korean [high-speed rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High-speed_rail) system, [Korea Train Express](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KTX) (KTX), provides high-speed service along the [Gyeongbu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gyeongbu_Line) and [Honam Lines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Honam_Line). Major cities such as [Seoul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seoul_Metropolitan_Subway), [Busan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Busan_Metro), and [Daegu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daegu_Metro) have urban rapid transit systems.[^cite_note-214] Express bus terminals are available in most cities.[^cite_note-215]

The main gateway and largest airport is [Incheon International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Incheon_International_Airport), serving 58 million passengers in 2016.[^cite_note-216] Other international airports include the [Gimpo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gimpo_International_Airport), [Busan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gimhae_International_Airport) and [Jeju](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeju_International_Airport) airports. There are also many airports that were built as part of the infrastructure boom but are barely used.[^cite_note-217] The national carrier [Korean Air](315411.md) served over 26 million passengers, including almost 19 million international passengers in 2016.[^cite_note-218] [Asiana Airlines](331239.md) also serves domestic and international traffic. Combined, South Korean airlines serve 297 international routes.[^cite_note-219] Smaller airlines, such as Jeju Air, provide domestic service with lower fares.[^cite_note-220]

### Energy

Main article: [Energy in South Korea](19356748.md)



Electricity generation in South Korea in terawatt hours

South Korea is the world's fifth-largest [nuclear power producer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_power_in_South_Korea) and the third-largest in Asia as of 2010.[^cite_note-koreanuke-221] Supplying 45% of its electricity production, nuclear research is very active with research into a variety of advanced reactors, including small modular reactors, liquid-metal fast/[transmutation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_transmutation#Artificial_transmutation_of_nuclear_waste) reactors and high-temperature [hydrogen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydrogen) generation reactors. Fuel production and waste handling technologies have also been developed locally. It is also a member of the [ITER](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ITER) project.[^cite_note-222]

South Korea is an emerging exporter of [nuclear reactors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_reactors), having concluded agreements with the United Arab Emirates to build and maintain four advanced nuclear reactors,[^cite_note-223] with [Jordan](7515964.md) for a research nuclear reactor,[^cite_note-224][^cite_note-225] and with Argentina for construction and repair of heavy-water nuclear reactors.[^cite_note-226][^cite_note-argentinanuke-227] As of 2010, South Korea and [Turkey](11125639.md) are in negotiations regarding construction of two nuclear reactors.[^cite_note-228] South Korea is also preparing to bid on construction of a light-water nuclear reactor for Argentina.[^cite_note-argentinanuke-227]

South Korea is not allowed to [enrich uranium](37555.md) or develop traditional uranium enrichment technology on its own due to U.S. political pressure,[^cite_note-229] unlike most major nuclear powers such as Japan, Germany, and France, competitors in the international nuclear market. This impediment to South Korea's indigenous nuclear industrial undertaking has sparked occasional diplomatic rows between the two allies. While successful in exporting its electricity-generating nuclear technology and nuclear reactors, it cannot capitalize on the [market for nuclear enrichment facilities and refineries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uranium_market), preventing it from further expanding its export niche. South Korea has sought unique technologies, such as [pyroprocessing](197845.md#Pyroprocessing), to circumvent these obstacles and gain a more advantageous competitive edge.[^cite_note-230] The U.S. has recently been wary of the burgeoning nuclear program, which South Korea insists will be for civilian use only.[^cite_note-koreanuke-221]

South Korea is the 2nd-highest-ranked Continental Asian country in the World Economic Forum's [Networked Readiness Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Networked_Readiness_Index), after Singapore—an indicator of a country's information and communication technology development level. South Korea ranks 9th worldwide.[^cite_note-231]

### Tourism

Main article: [Tourism in South Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_in_South_Korea)



Busan City Haeundae Beach

South Korean tourism is driven by many factors, including the prominence of Korean pop culture such as [South Korean pop music](629945.md) and [television dramas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_drama), known as the [Korean Wave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_Wave) or *Hallyu*, has gained popularity throughout the world. The Hyundai Research Institute reported that the Korean Wave has a direct influence on encouraging direct foreign investment back into the country through demand for products and the tourism industry.[^cite_note-Korea_Times-232] Among East Asian countries, China was the most receptive, investing \$1.4 billion in South Korea, with much of the investment within its service sector, a sevenfold increase from 2001. In 2019, more than 17 million foreign tourists visited South Korea.[^cite_note-233]

According to an analysis by economist Han Sang-Wan, a 1% increase in the exports of Korean cultural content pushes consumer goods exports up 0.083%, while a 1% increase in Korean pop content exports to a country produces a 0.019% bump in tourism.[^cite_note-Korea_Times-232]

Some private establishments in South Korea, particularly nightlife venues, maintain policies that restrict entry to Korean nationals or explicitly prohibit foreigners. Numerous reports have documented signs at bars and clubs stating "Koreans only" or "No foreigners allowed".[^cite_note-234][^cite_note-235]

### Welfare

Compared to other OECD members, South Korea spends less on welfare systems.[^cite_note-the_economist-2026-208] The [South Korean pension system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pension_policy_in_South_Korea) was created to provide benefits to persons reaching old age, families, and persons stricken with the death of their primary breadwinner, and for the purposes of stabilizing the nation's [welfare state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welfare_state).[^cite_note-Korea_Labor_Institute_1998-236] The structure is primarily based on taxation and is income-related.[^cite_note-237] The system is divided into four categories distributing benefits to participants through national, military personnel, governmental, and private school teacher pension schemes.[^cite_note-238] The national pension scheme is the primary welfare system providing allowances to the majority of persons. Eligibility for the national pension scheme is not dependent on income but on age and residence, with those aged 18 to 59 covered.[^cite_note-National_Pension_Service-239] Anyone under 18 is a dependent of someone who is covered or under a special exclusion where they are allowed to alternative provisions. The national pension scheme is divided into four categories of insured persons: the workplace-based insured, the individually insured, the voluntarily insured, and the voluntarily and continuously insured. An old-age pension scheme covers individuals age 60 or older for the rest of their lives as long as they have satisfied the minimum of 20 years of national pension coverage beforehand.[^cite_note-SSA.GOV-240]

### Science and technology

Main article: [History of science and technology in Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_science_and_technology_in_Korea)

See also: [List of Korean inventions and discoveries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Korean_inventions_and_discoveries)



A 3D [OLED](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OLED) TV made by Korean [LG Display](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LG_Display), the world's largest [LCD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LCD) and [OLED](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OLED) maker

Scientific and technological development in South Korea initially did not occur largely because of more pressing matters, such as the [division of Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Division_of_Korea) and the [Korean War](16772.md) that occurred right after its independence. It was not until the 1960s under the dictatorship of [Park Chung Hee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Park_Chung_Hee) that South Korea's economy rapidly grew from industrialization and the [chaebol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaebol) corporations such as [Samsung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samsung), [LG](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LG_Corporation), and [SK](1174665.md). Ever since the industrialization of South Korea's economy, South Korea has placed its focus on technology-based corporations, which have been supported by infrastructure developments by the government.

South Korea leads the OECD in graduates in science and engineering.[^cite_note-Business_Insider-241] From 2014 to 2019, the country ranked first among the most innovative countries in the [Bloomberg Innovation Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bloomberg_Innovation_Index).[^cite_note-242] It was ranked 4th in the [Global Innovation Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Innovation_Index) in 2025.[^cite_note-243][^cite_note-244] South Korea today is known as a launchpad of a mature mobile market that allows developers to reap benefits of a market where very few technology constraints exist. There is a growing trend of inventions of new types of media or apps, utilizing the 4G and 5G internet infrastructure in South Korea. South Korea has the infrastructure to meet a high density of population and culture; this, along with high revenues, allows South Korean-only tech startups to reach valuations of \$1 billion and above, a peak usually reserved for startups growing in several countries.[^cite_note-Tesla-245]

Total spending on research and development grew from about 3.9% of [gross domestic product](12594.md) (GDP) in 2013 to more than 4.9% in 2022, making it the second-highest in the world, behind only Israel, which spent 5.9%. In 2023, the government announced a spending cut by about 11% for 2024 and the intention to shift resources to new initiatives, such as efforts to build rockets, pursue biomedical research, and develop US-style biotech innovation.[^cite_note-246]

#### Cyber security

See also: [Internet censorship in South Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internet_censorship_in_South_Korea)

Following [cyberattacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_South_Korea_cyberattack) in the first half of 2013, in which government, news media, television stations, and banks' websites were compromised, the national government committed to training 5,000 new cybersecurity experts by 2017. The South Korean government [blamed North Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bureau_121) for these attacks, as well as incidents that occurred in 2009, 2011, and 2012, but Pyongyang denies the accusations.[^cite_note-Jun-247] South Korea's government maintains a broad-ranging approach toward the regulation of specific online content and imposes a substantial level of [censorship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Censorship_in_South_Korea) on election-related discourse and on many websites that the government deems subversive or socially harmful.[^cite_note-248][^cite_note-249]

#### Aerospace engineering

Main article: [Korea Aerospace Research Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korea_Aerospace_Research_Institute)



[KSLV-II Nuri](31909917.md) during liftoff

South Korea has sent up 10 satellites since 1992, all using foreign rockets and overseas launch pads, notably [Arirang-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arirang-1) in 1999, and [Arirang-2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arirang-2) in 2006, as part of its space partnership with Russia.[^cite_note-250] Arirang-1 was lost in space in 2008, after nine years in service.[^cite_note-251] In April 2008, [Yi So-yeon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yi_So-yeon) became the first Korean to fly in space, aboard the Russian [Soyuz TMA-12](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soyuz_TMA-12).[^cite_note-252][^cite_note-253]

In June 2009, the first [spaceport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spaceport) of South Korea, [Naro Space Center](3584260.md), was completed at [Goheung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goheung), [South Jeolla Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Jeolla_Province).[^cite_note-254] The launch of [Naro-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naro-1) in January 2013 was a success, after two previous failed attempts.[^cite_note-255]

Efforts to build an indigenous space launch vehicle have been marred by persistent political pressure from the United States, who had for many decades hindered South Korea's indigenous rocket and missile development programs[^cite_note-256] in fear of their possible connection to clandestine military ballistic missile programs, which Korea many times insisted did not violate the research and development guidelines stipulated by US-Korea agreements on restriction of rocket technology research and development.[^cite_note-257] South Korea has sought the assistance of foreign countries such as Russia through [MTCR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MTCR) commitments to supplement its restricted domestic rocket technology. The two failed KSLV-I [launch vehicles](1306157.md) were based on the [Universal Rocket Module](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_Rocket_Module), the first stage of the Russian [Angara rocket](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angara_rocket), combined with a solid-fueled second stage built by South Korea.

On October 21, 2021, the [KSLV-2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KSLV-2) Nuri was successfully launched, making South Korea a country with an indigenous orbital launch capability.[^cite_note-258]

#### Robotics

Main article: [South Korean robotics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Korean_robotics)



Albert HUBO, developed by [KAIST](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KAIST), can make expressive gestures with its five separate fingers.

[Robotics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robotics) has been included in the list of main national research and development projects since 2003.[^cite_note-259] In 2009, the government announced plans to build robot-themed parks in Incheon and Masan with a mix of public and private funding.[^cite_note-260] In 2005, Korea Advanced Institute of Science and Technology ([KAIST](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KAIST)) developed the world's second walking [humanoid robot](48180.md), [HUBO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HUBO). A team in the Korea Institute of Industrial Technology developed the first Korean [android](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Android_\(robot\)), [EveR-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EveR-1) in May 2006.[^cite_note-261] EveR-1 has been succeeded by more complex models with improved movement and vision.[^cite_note-262][^cite_note-263]

Plans of creating English-teaching robot assistants to compensate for the shortage of teachers were announced in February 2010, with the robots being deployed to most preschools and kindergartens by 2013.[^cite_note-264] Robotics are also incorporated in the entertainment sector; the *Korean Robot Game Festival* has been held every year since 2004 to promote science and robot technology.[^cite_note-265]

#### Biotechnology

Since the 1980s, the government has invested in the development of a domestic [biotechnology](4502.md) industry.[^cite_note-266] The medical sector accounts for a large part of the production, including production of [hepatitis vaccines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hepatitis_A_vaccine) and [antibiotics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antibiotic). Research and development in [genetics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetics) and [cloning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cloning) have received increasing attention, with the first successful cloning of a dog, [Snuppy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snuppy) in 2005, and the cloning of two females of an endangered species of [gray wolves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gray_wolves) by the [Seoul National University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seoul_National_University) in 2007.[^cite_note-267] The rapid growth of the industry has resulted in significant voids in regulation of ethics, as was highlighted by the [scientific misconduct](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hwang_Woo-Suk#Controversies) case involving [Hwang Woo-Suk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hwang_Woo-Suk).[^cite_note-268]

Since late 2020, SK Bioscience Inc. (a division of [SK Group](1174665.md)) has been producing a major proportion of the [Vaxzevria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxford%E2%80%93AstraZeneca_COVID-19_vaccine) vaccine (also known as COVID-19 Vaccine AstraZeneca), under license from the [University of Oxford](31797.md) and [AstraZeneca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AstraZeneca), for worldwide distribution through the [COVAX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVAX) facility under the [WHO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WHO) hospice. A recent agreement with [Novavax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Novavax) expands its production for a second vaccine to 40 million doses in 2022, with a \$450 million investment in domestic and overseas facilities.[^cite_note-269]

## Demographics

Main article: [Demographics of South Korea](27022.md)

See also: [Koreans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koreans) and [List of cities in South Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_in_South_Korea)



Population density of South Korea provinces

South Korea had an estimated population of roughly 51.7 million in 2022.[^cite_note-Kostat2016-270][^cite_note-KOSIS2015-271] The population more than doubled from 21.5 million in 1955[^cite_note-272] to 50 million by 2010.[^cite_note-chosun1-273] However, it was expected to peak at 52 million in 2024 and decline to 36 million by 2072,[^cite_note-newsweek-2024-274] owing to a rapid decline in birth rates that began in 1960. South Korea's birth rate became the world's lowest in 2009,[^cite_note-275] at an annual rate of approximately 9 births per 1000 people.[^cite_note-276] Fertility saw some modest increase afterwards,[^cite_note-277] but dropped to a new global low in 2017,[^cite_note-278] with fewer than 30,000 births per month for the first time since records began,[^cite_note-279] and less than one child per woman in 2018.[^cite_note-280] In 2020, the country recorded more deaths than births, resulting in the first population decrease since modern records began.[^cite_note-gladstone-2021-134][^cite_note-281]

By 2021, the fertility rate stood at just 0.81 children per woman,[^cite_note-282] well below the [replacement rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Replacement_rate) of 2.1, falling to 0.78 in 2022 and 0.72 in 2023 yet rising to 0.80 in 2025[^cite_note-283]—still the lowest in the world. Consequently, South Korea has seen the steepest decline in [working age population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Working-age) among [OECD](33853117.md) nations;[^cite_note-284] the proportion of people aged 65 years and over is slated to reach over 20% by 2025 and close to 45% by 2050.[^cite_note-285] The low birth rate has been declared a "national emergency" and prompted the creation of a new ministry in May 2024 dedicated to reversing the trend and addressing issues related to aging, immigration, and the workforce.[^cite_note-newsweek-2024-274] The government has also launched various incentives to help entice families to have children, including a cash allowance for newborns and greater funding of childcare and fertility treatments.[^cite_note-newsweek-2024-274]

Government policy, along with a rebound in marriages delayed by [COVID-19](63030231.md), may account for the Korean birth rate increasing in late 2024;[^cite_note-286] total births in the third quarter were up 8% from the same period last year, marking the largest quarterly increase since the third quarter of 2012 and the first annual rise in total fertility since 2015.[^cite_note-287] Data released in January 2025 show the number of births in November 2024 was 20,095, a 14.6% increase year-on-year, the highest growth rate since November 2010 (which recorded a 17.5% increase), and the third consecutive month of double-digit growth, following September's 10.1% and October's 13.4%.[^cite_note-288]

Most South Koreans live in urban areas, following rapid migration from the countryside during the country's rapid economic expansion in the 1970s through the 1990s.[^cite_note-289] About half the population (24.5 million) is concentrated in the [Seoul Metropolitan Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seoul_Metropolitan_Area), making it the world's second largest metropolitan area; other major cities include [Busan](68115.md) (3.5 million), [Incheon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Incheon) (3.0 million), [Daegu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daegu) (2.5 million), [Daejeon](284812.md) (1.4 million), [Gwangju](323128.md) (1.4 million) and [Ulsan](323111.md) (1.1 million).[^cite_note-290] Population density is estimated at 514.6 per square kilometre (1,333/sq mi) in 2022,[^cite_note-Kostat2016-270] more than 10 times the global average.



South Koreans in [traditional dress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanbok)

The population has been shaped by international migration. After World War II and the division of the Korean Peninsula, about four million people from North Korea crossed the border to South Korea. This trend of net entry reversed over the next 40 years because of emigration; large numbers of ethnic Koreans live overseas, sometimes in ethnic neighborhoods known as [Koreatowns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koreatown). The four largest diaspora populations are in [China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koreans_in_China) (2.3 million), the [United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_Americans) (1.8 million), [Japan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koreans_in_Japan) (850,000), and [Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_Canadians) (250,000).

South Korea is among the most [ethnically homogeneous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homogeneity_and_heterogeneity) societies in the world, with ethnic Koreans representing approximately 96% of the total population. Precise numbers are difficult to estimate since official statistics do not record ethnicity, and many [immigrants are ethnically Korean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_diaspora), while a growing number of South Korean citizens are not.[^cite_note-Korean_Statistical_Information_Service-291] The percentage of foreign nationals has been growing rapidly since the late 1990s,[^cite_note-292] with South Korea having one of the fastest-growing foreign-born populations: As of November 2023, there was an all-time high of 2.46 million foreign residents, accounting for nearly 5 percent of the total population, compared to 2016 figures of 1.4 million foreign residents (roughly 2.75 percent of the population). Much of this growth was driven by foreign workers and international students.[^cite_note-Korean_Statistical_Information_Service-291]

About 30,000 foreign-born residents obtain South Korean citizenship every year since 2010; in 2023, the number of foreigners who had acquired Korean nationality was 234,506, an increase of 4.8 percent from the prior year. The number of children of foreign residents born in South Korea increased by 7,809, or 2.8 percent, to 289,886.[^cite_note-293] Many foreign citizens are ethnic Koreans: [migrants from China (PRC)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_people_in_Korea) are the largest foreign-born group both proportionally and numerically, accounting for 56.5% of foreign nationals, but approximately 70% of these Chinese citizens are *[Joseonjok](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koreans_in_China)* (조선족), PRC citizens of Korean ethnicity.[^cite_note-ChosunIlbo20090806-294] In addition, about 43,000 English teachers from [English-speaking countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English-speaking_world) reside temporarily in Korea.[^cite_note-295]

Corresponding to its socioeconomic development, South Korea has experienced a dramatic increase in [life expectancy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life_expectancy), from 79.10 years in 2008[^cite_note-296] (which was [34th in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_life_expectancy)),[^cite_note-297] to 83.53 years in 2024—the fifth-highest of any country or territory.
