### Language

Main articles: [Korean language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_language) and [Korean dialects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_dialects)



Dialects of the Korean language

Korean is the official language of South Korea and is classified by most linguists as a [language isolate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Language_isolate). It incorporates a significant number of [loanwords](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loanword) from Chinese. Korean uses an indigenous writing system called [Hangul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hangul), created in 1446 by [Sejong the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sejong_the_Great) to provide a convenient alternative to the [Classical Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_Chinese) [Hanja](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanja) characters, which were difficult to learn and did not fit the Korean language well. South Korea still uses some Chinese Hanja characters in niche areas, such as print media and legal documentation.

The Korean language in South Korea has a [standard dialect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Korean_standard_language) known as the [Seoul dialect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gyeonggi_dialect), with an additional four dialects ([Chungcheong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chungcheong_dialect), [Gangwon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gangwon_dialect), [Gyeongsang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gyeongsang_dialect), and [Jeolla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeolla_dialect)) and one language ([Jeju](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeju_language)) in use around the country. Almost all South Korean students today learn [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_language_education_in_South_Korea) throughout their education.[^cite_note-299][^cite_note-300]

### Religion

Main article: [Religion in South Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_South_Korea)

Religion in South Korea (2024)

[^cite_note-2024_religion-4]

1. [No religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreligion) (51.0%)

2. [Protestantism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism)  (20.0%)

3. [Catholicism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_in_Korea)  (11.0%)

4. [Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_in_South_Korea)  (17.0%)

5. Other religions (2.00%)

According to the 2024 results of the Korea Research's regular survey "Public Opinion Within Public Opinion", more than half of the South Korean population (51%) declared themselves not affiliated with any [religious organizations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organized_religion).[^cite_note-2024_religion-4] Of the people who are affiliated with a religious organization, most are [Christians](5211.md) and [Buddhists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_Buddhism). According to the survey, 31% of the population were Christians (20% identified themselves as Protestants, 11% as Roman Catholics) and 17% were Buddhists.[^cite_note-2024_religion-4] Other religions include [Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_South_Korea) (approximately 130,000 Muslims, which includes 73% of migrant workers from Pakistan and Bangladesh and 27% being Korean Muslims),[^cite_note-301] the homegrown sect of [Won Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Won_Buddhism), and a variety of indigenous religions, including [Cheondoism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cheondoism) (a [Confucianizing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confucianism) religion), [Jeungsanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeungsanism), [Daejongism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daejongism), [Daesun Jinrihoe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daesun_Jinrihoe), and others. [Freedom of religion](46006.md) is guaranteed by the constitution, and there is no [state religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_religion).[^cite_note-302] Overall, between 2015 and 2024, there has been a slight rise in Christianity (from 27.6% to 31%), a slow rise in Buddhism (from 15.5% to 17%), and a decline in the unaffiliated population (from 56.9% to 51%).[^cite_note-2024_religion-4][^cite_note-2015_Census-303][^cite_note-ABC-CLIO-304]



The [Myeongdong Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Myeongdong_Cathedral), seat of the [Archdiocese of Seoul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archdiocese_of_Seoul).

Christianity is South Korea's largest organized religion, accounting for more than half of all South Korean adherents of religious organizations. There are approximately 16 million Christians in South Korea today; about two-thirds of them belong to Protestant churches, and the rest to the Catholic Church.[^cite_note-2024_religion-4] The number of Protestants had been stagnant throughout the 1990s and the 2000s but increased to a peak level throughout the 2010s. Roman Catholics increased significantly between the 1980s and the 2000s but declined throughout the 2010s.[^cite_note-2024_religion-4] Christianity, unlike in other East Asian countries, found fertile ground in Korea in the 18th century, and by the end of the 18th century, it persuaded a large part of the population, as the declining monarchy supported it and opened the country to widespread [proselytism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proselytism) as part of a project of Westernization. The weakness of [Korean shamanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_shamanism), which—unlike Japanese [Shinto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shinto) and [China's religious system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_folk_religion)—never developed into a national religion of high status,[^cite_note-305] combined with the impoverished state of [Korean Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_Buddhism), (after 500 years of suppression at the hands of the Joseon state, by the 20th century it was virtually extinct), left a free hand to Christian churches. Christianity's similarity to native religious narratives has been studied as another factor that contributed to its success in the peninsula.[^cite_note-306] The [Japanese colonization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korea_under_Japanese_rule) of the first half of the 20th century further strengthened the identification of Christianity with [Korean nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_nationalism), as the Japanese coopted native Korean shamanism into the Nipponic [Imperial Shinto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shinto_in_Korea) that they tried to establish in the peninsula.[^cite_note-Korean_Social_Sciences_Journal-307] Widespread Christianization of the Koreans took place during State Shinto,[^cite_note-Korean_Social_Sciences_Journal-307] after its abolition, and then in the independent South Korea as the newly established military government supported Christianity and [tried to oust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-shamanism_movement_in_Korea) native shamanism.



[Buddha's Birthday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddha's_Birthday) celebration in Seoul

Among Christian denominations, [Presbyterianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presbyterianism_in_South_Korea) is the largest. About nine million people belong to one of the hundred different Presbyterian churches; the biggest ones are the [HapDong Presbyterian Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presbyterian_Church_in_Korea_\(HapDong\)), [TongHap Presbyterian Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presbyterian_Church_of_Korea_\(TongHap\)), and the [Koshin Presbyterian Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kosin_Presbyterian_Church_in_Korea). South Korea is also the second-largest missionary-sending nation, after the United States.[^cite_note-308]

Buddhism was introduced to Korea in the 4th century.[^cite_note-309] It soon became a dominant religion in the southeastern kingdom of Silla, the region that hitherto hosts the strongest concentration of Buddhists in South Korea. In the other states of the Three Kingdoms Period, Goguryeo and Baekje, it became the state religion in 372 and 528, respectively. It remained the state religion in Later Silla and Goryeo. It was later suppressed throughout much of the subsequent history under the unified kingdom of Joseon, which officially adopted a strict [Korean Confucianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_Confucianism). Today, South Korea has about 8.7 million Buddhists,[^cite_note-2024_religion-4] most of them affiliated to the [Jogye Order](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jogye_Order). Most of the [National Treasures of South Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Treasure_\(South_Korea\)) are Buddhist artifacts.

### Education

Main article: [Education in South Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_South_Korea)



[Seoul National University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seoul_National_University) is considered to be the most prestigious university in South Korea.

A centralized administration in South Korea oversees the process for the education of children from kindergarten to the third and final year of high school. The school year is divided into two semesters: the first begins in March and ends in mid-July, and the second begins in late August and ends in mid-February. The country adopted a new educational program to increase the number of international students by 2010. According to the [Ministry of Education, Science and Technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Education,_Science_and_Technology_\(South_Korea\)), the number of scholarships for international students in South Korea would have (under the program) doubled by that time, and the number of international students would have reached 100,000.[^cite_note-310]

South Korea is one of the top-performing [Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organisation_for_Economic_Co-operation_and_Development) (OECD) countries in reading literacy, mathematics, and sciences, with the average student scoring 519, compared with the OECD average of 492, placing it ninth in the world. The country has one of the world's highest-educated labor forces among OECD countries.[^cite_note-311][^cite_note-312] The country is well known for its highly feverish outlook on education, where its national obsession with education has been called "education fever".[^cite_note-313][^cite_note-314][^cite_note-315] This obsession with education has catapulted the resource-poor nation consistently atop the global education rankings. In 2014, South Korea ranked second worldwide (after Singapore) in the national rankings of students' math and science scores by the OECD.[^cite_note-316] Higher education is a serious issue in South Korean society, where it is viewed as one of the fundamental cornerstones of South Korean life. Education is regarded with a high priority for South Korean families, as success in education is often a source of honor and pride for families and within South Korean society at large, and is seen as a fundamental necessity to channel one's [social mobility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_mobility) to ultimately improve one's socioeconomic position in South Korean society.[^cite_note-317][^cite_note-318] Due to the importance of education in Korean society, many students attend [cram schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hagwon).[^cite_note-319]



[KAIST](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KAIST) main campus in [Daejeon](284812.md)&#x20;

In 2015, the country spent 5.1% of its GDP on education at all levels—roughly 0.8 percentage points above the OECD average of 4.3%.[^cite_note-320] A strong investment in education, a militant drive to achieve academic success, as well as the passion for scholarly excellence has helped the country rapidly grow its economy over the past 60 years into a developed country.[^cite_note-ICEF-321]

As of 2025, the school violence victimization rate reached 2.5%, the highest level since the government began tracking the data in 2013. Data shows that in South Korea, bullies often outnumber victims by three or four to one because an entire class may participate in ostracizing a single student. Elementary school students now report the highest victimization rates, at approximately 5.0%.[^cite_note-322][^cite_note-323]

### Health

Main article: [Health in South Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_in_South_Korea)



Development of life expectancy in North Korea and South Korea

South Korea has a [universal health care](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_health_care) system.[^cite_note-324] According to Health Care Index rankings, it has the world's second-best healthcare system.[^cite_note-325] South Korean hospitals have advanced medical equipment and facilities readily available, ranking 4th for [MRI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MRI) units per capita and 6th for [CT scanners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CT_scanner) per capita in the OECD.[^cite_note-326] It also had the OECD's [second largest number of hospital beds per 1000 people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_hospital_beds) at 9.56 beds. [Life expectancy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life_expectancy) has been rising rapidly and South Korea [ranked 6th in the world for life expectancy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_life_expectancy) at 83.5 years in 2023.[^cite_note-327] It also has the third-highest health-adjusted life expectancy in the world.[^cite_note-328] South Korea maintains the world's highest per capita rate of [plastic surgery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plastic_surgery).[^cite_note-329][^cite_note-330] [Suicide in South Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suicide_in_South_Korea) is the [12th highest in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_suicide_rate) according to the [World Health Organization](33583.md), as well as the highest suicide rate in the [OECD](33853117.md), a position it has held for most of the 21st century.[^cite_note-331][^cite_note-332][^cite_note-333][^cite_note-334]

## Culture

Main article: [Culture of South Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_South_Korea)

See also: [Culture of Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Korea)



A musician playing a *[gayageum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gayageum)*

South Korea shares its traditional culture with North Korea, but the two Koreas have developed distinct contemporary forms of culture since the peninsula was divided in 1945. Historically, while the culture has been [heavily influenced by that of neighboring China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_influence_on_Korean_culture), it has nevertheless independently managed to develop a unique cultural identity in its own right that is distinct from its larger neighbors.[^cite_note-335] As of 2024, South Korea has 24 [UNESCO Intangible Cultural Heritages of Humanity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UNESCO_Intangible_Cultural_Heritage_Lists),[^cite_note-336] along with [16 World Heritage Sites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_World_Heritage_Sites_in_South_Korea).[^cite_note-337] The [Ministry of Culture, Sports and Tourism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Culture,_Sports_and_Tourism_\(South_Korea\)) actively encourages the traditional arts, as well as modern forms, through funding and education programs.[^cite_note-338] According to the 2023 edition of the [Press Freedom Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Press_Freedom_Index), South Korea has the second highest level of press freedom in Continental and East Asia, behind [Taiwan](25734.md).[^cite_note-339]

Industrialization and urbanization have brought many changes to the way modern Koreans live. Changing economic circumstances and lifestyles have led to a concentration of population in major cities, especially the capital Seoul, with multi-generational households separating into [nuclear family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_family) living arrangements. A 2014 [Euromonitor International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euromonitor_International) study on 44 countries found that South Koreans [drink the most alcohol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_alcohol_consumption_per_capita) on a weekly basis compared to the rest of the world. South Koreans drink 13.7 shots of liquor per week on average, with [Russia](25391.md), the [Philippines](23440.md), and [Thailand](30128.md) following.[^cite_note-340]

### Art

Main article: [Korean art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_art)



A blue and white porcelain peach-shaped water dropper from the Joseon dynasty in the 18th century

Korean art has been highly influenced by [Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_Buddhism) and [Confucianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_Confucianism), which can be seen in the many traditional paintings, sculptures, ceramics and the performing arts.[^cite_note-341] [Korean pottery and porcelain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_pottery_and_porcelain), such as Joseon's *[baekja](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseon_white_porcelain)* and [buncheong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buncheong), and [Goryeo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goryeo)'s [celadon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celadon) are well known throughout the world.[^cite_note-342] The [Korean tea ceremony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_tea_ceremony), *[pansori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pansori)*, *[talchum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talchum)*, and *[buchaechum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buchaechum)* are also notable Korean performing arts.

Post-war modern Korean art started to flourish in the 1960s and 1970s, when South Korean artists took an interest in geometrical shapes and intangible subjects. Establishing harmony between [man and nature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Man_and_nature) was also a favorite of this time. Due to social instability, social issues became the main subjects in the 1980s. Art was influenced by various international events and exhibits in Korea, which brought more diversity.[^cite_note-343] The [Olympic Sculpture Garden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olympic_Park,_Seoul) in 1988, the transposition of the 1993 edition of the [Whitney Biennial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whitney_Biennial) to Seoul,[^cite_note-344] the creation of the [Gwangju Biennale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gwangju_Biennale)[^cite_note-345] and the Korean Pavilion at the [Venice Biennale](301876.md) in 1995[^cite_note-346] were notable events.

### Architecture

Main article: [Architecture of South Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_South_Korea)

See also: [Korean architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_architecture)



Buyongjeong area in [Changdeokgung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Changdeokgung)

Because of South Korea's tumultuous history, construction and destruction have been repeated endlessly, resulting in an interesting melange of architectural styles and designs.[^cite_note-347]

Traditional Korean architecture is characterized by its harmony with nature. Ancient architects adopted the [bracket system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bracket_\(architecture\)) characterized by [thatched roofs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thatching) and heated floors called *[ondol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ondol)*.[^cite_note-348] People of the upper classes built bigger houses with elegantly curved tiled roofs and lifting eaves. Traditional architecture can be seen in the palaces and temples, preserved old houses called *[hanok](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanok)*,[^cite_note-349] and special sites like [Hahoe Folk Village](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hahoe_Folk_Village), [Yangdong Folk Village](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yangdong_Folk_Village) and [Korean Folk Village](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_Folk_Village). Traditional architecture may also be seen at several of the [UNESCO World Heritage Sites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_World_Heritage_Sites_in_South_Korea).[^cite_note-350]



[Government-General of Chōsen Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government-General_of_Ch%C5%8Dsen_Building) was demolished in 1996 under the [Kim Young-sam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kim_Young-sam) government.

Western architecture was first introduced at the end of the 19th century. Churches, offices for foreign legislation, schools, and university buildings were built in new styles. With Japan's annexation of Korea in 1910, the colonial regime intervened in Korea's architectural heritage, imposing [Japanese-style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_architecture) modern architecture. Anti-Japanese sentiment and the Korean War led to the destruction of most buildings constructed during that time.[^cite_note-351]

Modern Korean architecture entered a new phase of development during the post-Korean War reconstruction, incorporating modern architectural trends and styles. Stimulated by the economic growth in the 1970s and 1980s, active redevelopment saw new horizons in architectural design. In the aftermath of the 1988 Seoul Olympics, South Korea has witnessed a wide variety of styles in its architectural landscape, with the opening of the market to foreign architects.[^cite_note-352] Contemporary architectural efforts have attempted to balance the traditional philosophy of "harmony with nature" and the fast-paced urbanization that the country has been going through in recent years.[^cite_note-353]

### Entertainment

Main articles: [Cinema of South Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_South_Korea), [Korean drama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_drama), [Korean Wave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_Wave), [K-pop](629945.md), and [Mass media in South Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_media_in_South_Korea)



K-pop group [BTS](39862325.md) has emerged as one of the country's most successful Korean boy bands since their rise to international prominence during the latter half of the 2010s.

In addition to domestic consumption, South Korea has a thriving entertainment industry, in which television dramas, films, and popular music have garnered international popularity and generated significant export revenues for the nation's economy. The cultural phenomenon known as *[Hallyu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_Wave)*, or the "Korean Wave", has swept many countries across Continental and East Asia, making South Korea a major soft power as an exporter of popular culture and entertainment, rivaling Western nations such as the United States and the United Kingdom.[^cite_note-Kwave-354][^cite_note-355][^cite_note-356][^cite_note-357][^cite_note-358]



[Blackpink](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blackpink) has been one of the most popular K-pop girl groups in the world since the late 2010s.

Until the 1990s, [trot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trot_\(music\)) and traditional [folk-based](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditional_music_of_Korea) ballads dominated the South Korean popular music scene. The emergence of the pop group [Seo Taiji and Boys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seo_Taiji_and_Boys) in 1992 marked a turning point for South Korean popular music, also known as [K-pop](629945.md). Since the 1990s, the genre of K-pop has continuously engaged in a process of ongoing reinvention and modernization by assimilating various elements of popular musical genres and trends from across the world such as [Western popular music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pop_music), [experimental](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Experimental_music), [jazz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jazz), [gospel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gospel_music), [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_music_\(genre\)), [hip-hop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hip-hop), [rhythm and blues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contemporary_R\&B), [electronic dance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electronic_dance), [reggae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reggae), [country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Country_music), [folk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contemporary_folk_music), and [rock](25423.md) on top of its uniquely [traditional Korean music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditional_Korean_music) roots.[^cite_note-359] Although Western-style pop, hip-hop, rhythm and blues, rock, folk, and electronic dance-oriented acts have become dominant in the contemporary South Korean popular music scene, trot continues to be appreciated and enjoyed by older South Koreans. [K-pop idols](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_idol) are well known across Continental Asia, have found fame in the Western World, and have generated millions of dollars in export revenue beyond the confines of the traditional [East Asian music market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_industry_of_East_Asia#Ranking). Many K-pop acts have also established themselves by securing a strong global following using online social media platforms such as [YouTube](3524766.md). K-pop first began to make its mark outside of Continental and East Asia following the unexpected success of singer [Psy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psy)'s international music sensation, "[Gangnam Style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gangnam_Style)", which topped global music charts in 2012.

Since the success of the film *[Shiri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shiri_\(film\))* in 1999, the [Korean film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_Korea) industry has grown substantially, garnering recognition both nationwide and across the globe. Domestic films have a dominant share of the South Korean film market, partly because of the existence of government [screen quotas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Screen_quotas) requiring cinemas to show Korean films for at least 73 days of the year.[^cite_note-360] 2019's *[Parasite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parasite_\(2019_film\))*, directed by [Bong Joon-ho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bong_Joon-ho), became the [highest-grossing film in South Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_highest-grossing_films_in_South_Korea) as well as the first non-English language film to win Best Picture at the United States-based [Academy Awards](324.md) [that year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/92nd_Academy_Awards) amongst [numerous other accolades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_accolades_received_by_Parasite).



People [cosplaying](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cosplay) as guards from *[Squid Game](68455171.md)*

[South Korean television shows](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Television_in_South_Korea) have become popular outside of Korea. Television dramas, known as [K-dramas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_drama), have begun to find fame internationally. Many dramas tend to have a romantic focus. Historical dramas are also famous.[^cite_note-361][^cite_note-362] The 2021 survival drama *[Squid Game](68455171.md)*, created by [Hwang Dong-hyuk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hwang_Dong-hyuk), received critical acclaim and widespread international attention upon its release, becoming [Netflix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Netflix)'s most-watched series at launch and garnering a viewership of more than 142 million households during its first four weeks from launch.[^cite_note-363][^cite_note-364][^cite_note-365][^cite_note-366]

Unlike many other countries, South Korea's cultural exports, including K-pop and television dramas, are integrated into a formal state development strategy. The South Korean government allocates substantial annual funding exceeding several billion dollars to foster K-content as a tool for national branding and soft power. This systematic investment aims to project a modernized image of the country and stimulate economic growth through increased tourism and cultural exports.[^cite_note-367][^cite_note-368]

### Holidays

Main article: [Public holidays in South Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_holidays_in_South_Korea)

The [Korean New Year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_New_Year), or "Seollal", is celebrated on the first day of the [Korean calendar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_calendar). Korean Independence Day falls on March 1 and commemorates the [March First Movement](430481.md) of 1919. [Memorial Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Memorial_Day_\(South_Korea\)) is celebrated on June 6, and its purpose is to honor the men and women who died in South Korea's independence movement. [Constitution Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_Day_\(South_Korea\)) is on July 17, and it celebrates the promulgation of [Constitution of the Republic of Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_South_Korea). [Liberation Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Liberation_Day_of_Korea), on August 15, celebrates Korea's liberation from the [Empire of Japan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empire_of_Japan) in 1945.

Every 15th day of the 8th lunar month, Koreans celebrate the [Midautumn Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chuseok), in which Koreans visit their ancestral hometowns and eat a variety of traditional Korean foods. On October 1, Armed Forces Day is celebrated, honoring the military forces of South Korea. October 3 is [National Foundation Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaecheonjeol). [Hangul Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hangul_Day) on October 9 commemorates the invention of [hangul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hangul), the native alphabet of the [Korean language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_language).

### Cuisine

Main articles: [Korean cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_cuisine) and [South Korean cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Korean_cuisine)



[Bibimbap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bibimbap)

Korean cuisine, *hanguk yori*, or *hansik*, has evolved through centuries of social and political change. Ingredients and dishes vary by province. There are many significant regional dishes that have proliferated in different variations across the country in the present day. The [Korean royal court cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_royal_court_cuisine) once brought all of the unique regional specialties together for the royal family. Meals consumed both by the royal family and ordinary citizens have been regulated by a unique culture of etiquette.

Korean cuisine is largely based on [rice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rice), [noodles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noodle), [tofu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tofu), vegetables, fish and meats. Traditional meals are noted for the number of side dishes, *[banchan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banchan)*, which accompany steam-cooked short-grain rice. Every meal is accompanied by numerous banchan. [Kimchi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kimchi), a fermented, usually spicy vegetable dish, is commonly served at every meal and is one of the best-known dishes. Korean cuisine usually involves heavy seasoning with [sesame oil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sesame_oil), *[doenjang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doenjang)* (a type of [fermented soybean paste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fermented_bean_paste)), [soy sauce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soy_sauce), salt, garlic, ginger, and *[gochujang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gochujang)* (a hot pepper paste). Other well-known dishes are *[bulgogi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulgogi)*, grilled marinated beef; *[gimbap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gimbap)*; and *[tteokbokki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tteokbokki)*, a spicy snack consisting of rice cake seasoned with gochujang or a spicy chili paste.

Soups are also a common part of a meal and are served as part of the main course rather than at the beginning or the end of the meal. Soups known as *[guk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guk)* are often made with meats, shellfish and vegetables. Similar to guk, *tang* has less water and is more often served in restaurants. Another type is *[jjigae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jjigae)*, a [stew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stew) that is typically heavily seasoned with chili pepper and served boiling hot.

Popular [Korean alcoholic drinks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_alcoholic_drinks) include *[Soju](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soju)*, *[Makgeolli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Makgeolli)* and *[Bokbunja ju](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bokbunja_ju)*. Korea is unique among East Asian countries in its use of metal [chopsticks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chopsticks). Metal chopsticks have been discovered in Goguryeo archaeological sites.[^cite_note-369]

### Sports

Main article: [Sport in South Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_in_South_Korea)



[Seoul Sports Complex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seoul_Sports_Complex), Korea's largest integrated sports center

The [martial art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martial_arts) [taekwondo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taekwondo) originated in Korea. In the 1950s and 1960s, modern rules were standardized, with taekwondo becoming an official [Olympic sport](22576.md) in 2000.[^cite_note-370] Other Korean martial arts include [Taekkyon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taekkyeon), [hapkido](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hapkido), [Tang Soo Do](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tang_Soo_Do), [Kuk Sool Won](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuk_Sool_Won), [kumdo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kumdo) and [subak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subak).[^cite_note-371]

[Football](10568.md) has traditionally been regarded as the most popular sport in Korea, with [baseball](3850.md) as the second.[^cite_note-372] Recent polling indicates that a majority, 41% of South Korean sports fans continue to self-identify as football fans, with baseball ranked 2nd at 25% of respondents. However, the polling did not indicate the extent to which respondents follow both sports.[^cite_note-373]

The [national football team](1018627.md) became the first team in the [Asian Football Confederation](715249.md) to reach the [FIFA World Cup](11370.md) semi-finals in the [2002 FIFA World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2002_FIFA_World_Cup), jointly hosted by South Korea and Japan. The [Korea Republic national team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korea_Republic_national_football_team) (as it is known) has qualified for every World Cup since [Mexico 1986](157231.md), and has broken out of the group stage in 2002, in [2010](168079.md), when it was defeated by eventual semi-finalist [Uruguay](679782.md) in the Round of 16, and in [2022](17742072.md). At the [2012 Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Summer_Olympics), South Korea won the bronze medal for football.



[Sajik Baseball Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sajik_Baseball_Stadium) in [Busan](68115.md). Baseball is one of the most popular sports in South Korea.

Baseball was first introduced to Korea in 1905.[^cite_note-374][^cite_note-375][^cite_note-376] Recent years have been characterized by increasing attendance and ticket prices for professional baseball games.[^cite_note-377][^cite_note-378] The [Korea Professional Baseball](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korea_Professional_Baseball) league, a 10-team circuit, was established in 1982. The [South Korea national team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Korea_national_baseball_team) finished third in the [2006 World Baseball Classic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_World_Baseball_Classic) and second in the [2009 tournament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009_World_Baseball_Classic). The team's 2009 final game against Japan was widely watched in Korea, with a large screen at [Gwanghwamun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gwanghwamun) crossing in Seoul broadcasting the game live.[^cite_note-379] In the [2008 Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Summer_Olympics), South Korea won the gold medal in baseball.[^cite_note-380] Also in 1982, at the Baseball World Cup, Korea won the gold medal. At the [2010 Asian Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Asian_Games), the Korean National Baseball team won the gold medal. Several Korean players have gone on to play in [Major League Baseball](38776.md).

[Basketball](3921.md) is a popular sport in the country as well. South Korea has traditionally had one of the top basketball teams in Asia and one of the continent's strongest basketball divisions. Seoul hosted the [1967](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1967_ABC_Championship) and [1995 Asian Basketball Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1995_ABC_Championship). The [Korea national basketball team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korea_national_basketball_team) has won a record number of 23 medals at the event to date.[^cite_note-381]



[Taekwondo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taekwondo), a Korean martial art and Olympic sport

South Korea hosted the [Asian Games](39205.md) in 1986 (Seoul), 2002 (Busan), and 2014 (Incheon). It also hosted the Winter [Universiade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universiade) in 1997, the [Asian Winter Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_Winter_Games) in 1999, and the Summer Universiade in 2003 and 2015. In 1988, South Korea hosted the [Summer Olympics in Seoul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1988_Summer_Olympics), coming fourth with 12 gold medals, 10 silver medals, and 11 bronze medals. South Korea regularly performs well in [archery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archery), [shooting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shooting_sports), [table tennis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Table_tennis), [badminton](3956.md), [short track speed skating](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Short_track_speed_skating), [handball](13730.md), [field hockey](10886.md), [freestyle wrestling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freestyle_wrestling), [Greco-Roman wrestling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greco-Roman_wrestling), [baseball](3850.md), [judo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judo), [taekwondo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taekwondo), [speed skating](28028.md), [figure skating](11152.md), and [weightlifting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olympic_weightlifting). The [Seoul Olympic Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seoul_Olympic_Museum) is dedicated to the 1988 Summer Olympics.

[Pyeongchang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pyeongchang_County) hosted the [2018 Winter Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Winter_Olympics). South Korea has won more medals in the [Winter Olympics](34069.md) than any other Asian country, with a total of 45 (23 gold, 14 silver, and 8 bronze). At the [2010 Winter Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Winter_Olympics), South Korea ranked fifth in the overall medal rankings. South Korea is especially strong in [short track speed skating](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Short_track_speed_skating). [Speed skating](28028.md) and [figure skating](11152.md) are also popular, and [ice hockey](14790.md) is an emerging sport, with [Anyang Halla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anyang_Halla) winning their first ever [Asia League Ice Hockey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asia_League_Ice_Hockey) title in March 2010.[^cite_note-382]



[Seoul World Cup Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seoul_World_Cup_Stadium) in [Seoul](19159283.md) with a capacity of 66,704 seats

Seoul hosted a professional [triathlon](31524.md) race, which is part of the [International Triathlon Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Triathlon_Union) (ITU) World Championship Series in 2010.[^cite_note-383] In 2011, the South Korean city of Daegu hosted the [2011 IAAF World Championships in Athletics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_World_Championships_in_Athletics).[^cite_note-384] In 2010, South Korea hosted its first [Formula One](10854.md) race at the [Korea International Circuit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korea_International_Circuit) in [Yeongam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yeongam).[^cite_note-385] The [Korean Grand Prix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_Grand_Prix) was held from 2010 to 2013.[^cite_note-386]

Domestic [horse racing](74711.md) events are followed by South Koreans and [Seoul Race Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LetsRun_Park_Seoul) in [Gwacheon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gwacheon), [Gyeonggi Province](323103.md) is located closest to Seoul out of the country's three tracks.[^cite_note-387]

Competitive [video gaming](5363.md), better known as [esports](564204.md), has become more popular in South Korea in recent years, particularly among young people.[^cite_note-388] The two most popular games are *[League of Legends](21838352.md)* and *[StarCraft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/StarCraft)*. The gaming scene is managed by the [Korean e-Sports Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_e-Sports_Association).

Numerous ministries and agencies had relocated to Sejong. However, based on [Constitutional Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_Court_of_Korea) decision in 2004, complete capital relocation from Seoul requires a constitutional referendum. As of 2026, Seoul remains the seat of the [Blue House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_House), [National Assembly](601423.md), [Supreme Court](4899540.md), and Constitutional Court.

[^cite_note-11]: &#x20;See [Date and time notation in South Korea ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Date_and_time_notation_in_South_Korea).

[^cite_note-12]: &#x20;South Koreans use the name *Hanguk* (한국; 韓國) when referring to South Korea or Korea as a whole. The literal translation of South Korea, *Namhan* (남한; 南韓), is rarely used. [Until a change in policy in 2024](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Two_hostile_states_theory), the official line in North Korea was to use the name *Namjosŏn* (*Namjoseon*; 남조선; 南朝鮮) when referring to South Korea, derived from the North Korean name for Korea, *Chosŏn* (*Joseon*; 조선; 朝鮮). The North Korean state now uses *Hanguk* when referring to South Korea.

[^cite_note-unloved-28]: &#x20;[Myers, Brian Reynolds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brian_Reynolds_Myers) (December 28, 2016). ["Still the Unloved Republic"](https://web.archive.org/web/20180313210134/http://sthelepress.com/index.php/2016/12/28/still-the-unloved-republic/). *Sthele Press*. Archived from [the original](http://sthelepress.com/index.php/2016/12/28/still-the-unloved-republic/) on March 13, 2018. Retrieved June 10, 2019. *Taehan minguk*. In English it is translated as Republic of Korea or South Korea, names which to us foreigners denote the state as a political entity distinct from its northern neighbor. To most people here, however, *Taehan minguk* conveys that sense only when used in contrastive proximity with the word *Pukhan* (North Korea). Ask South Koreans when the *Taehan minguk* was established; more will answer '5000 years ago' than 'in 1948,' because to them it is simply the full name for *Hanguk*, Korea, the homeland. That's all it meant to most people who shouted those four syllables so proudly during the World Cup in 2002.

[*A New History of Korea*](https://books.google.com/books?id=g2mdVwXpMzwC\&pg=PA47). Harvard University Press. p. 47. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-0-674-61576-2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-0-674-61576-2). [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20230114234931/https://books.google.com/books?id=g2mdVwXpMzwC\&pg=PA47) from the original on January 14, 2023. Retrieved July 29, 2016. Koguryŏ was the first to open hostilities, with a bold assault across the Liao River against Liao-hsi, in 598. The Sui emperor, Wen Ti, launched a retaliatory attack on Koguryŏ but met with reverses and turned back in mid-course. Yang Ti, the next Sui emperor, proceeded in 612 to mount an invasion of unprecedented magnitude, marshalling a huge force said to number over a million men. And when his armies failed to take Liao-tung Fortress (modern Liao-yang), the anchor of Koguryŏ's first line of defense, he had a nearly a third of his forces, some 300,000 strong, break off the battle there and strike directly at the Koguryŏ capital of P'yŏngyang. But the Sui army was lured into a trap by the famed Koguryŏ commander Ŭlchi Mundŏk, and suffered a calamitous defeat at the Salsu (Ch'ŏngch'ŏn) River. It is said that only 2,700 of the 300,000 Sui soldiers who had crossed the Yalu survived to find their way back, and the Sui emperor now lifted the siege of Liao-tung Fortress and withdrew his forces to China proper. Yang Ti continued to send his armies against Koguryŏ but again without success, and before long his war-weakened empire crumbled. Nahm, Andrew C. (2005). [*A Panorama of 5000 Years: Korean History*](https://archive.org/details/panoramaof5000ye0000nahm/page/18) (Second revised ed.). Seoul: Hollym International Corporation. p. [18](https://archive.org/details/panoramaof5000ye0000nahm/page/18). [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-0-930878-68-9](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-0-930878-68-9). China, which had been split into many states since the early 3rd century, was reunified by the Sui dynasty at the end of the 6th century. Soon after that, Sui China mobilized a large number of troops and launched war against Koguryŏ. However, the people of Koguryŏ were united and they were able to repel the Chinese aggressors. In 612, Sui troops invaded Korea again, but Koguryŏ forces fought bravely and destroyed Sui troops everywhere. General Ŭlchi Mundŏk of Koguryŏ completely wiped out some 300,000 Sui troops which came across the Yalu River in the battles near the Salsu River (now Ch'ŏngch'ŏn River) with his ingenious military tactics. Only 2,700 Sui troops were able to flee from Korea. The Sui dynasty, which wasted so much energy and manpower in aggressive wars against Koguryŏ, fell in 618.

From what Ennin tells us, it seems that commerce between East China, Korea and Japan was, for the most part, in the hands of men from Silla. Here in the relatively dangerous waters on the eastern fringes of the world, they performed the same functions as did the traders of the placid Mediterranean on the western fringes. This is a historical fact of considerable significance but one which has received virtually no attention in the standard historical compilations of that period or in the modern books based on these sources. ... While there were limits to the influence of the Koreans along the eastern coast of China, there can be no doubt of their dominance over the waters off these shores. ... The days of Korean maritime dominance in the Far East actually were numbered, but in Ennin's time the men of Silla were still the masters of the seas in their part of the world. `{{cite book}}`: ISBN / Date incompatibility ([help](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:CS1_errors#invalid_isbn_date))

When Parhae perished at the hands of the Khitan around this same time, much of its ruling class, who were of Koguryŏ descent, fled to Koryŏ. Wang Kŏn warmly welcomed them and generously gave them land. Along with bestowing the name Wang Kye ("Successor of the Royal Wang") on the Parhae crown prince, Tae Kwang-hyŏn, Wang Kŏn entered his name in the royal household register, thus clearly conveying the idea that they belonged to the same lineage, and also had rituals performed in honor of his progenitor. Thus Koryŏ achieved a true national unification that embraced not only the Later Three Kingdoms but even survivors of Koguryŏ lineage from the Parhae kingdom.

[^cite_note-Kim_p.45-111]: &#x20;Kim, Samuel S. (2014). "The Evolving Asian System". *International Relations of Asia*. [Rowman & Littlefield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rowman_&_Littlefield). p. 45. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-1-4422-2641-8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-1-4422-2641-8). With three of the four major Cold War fault lines—divided Germany, divided Korea, divided China, and divided Vietnam—East Asia acquired the dubious distinction of having engendered the largest number of armed conflicts resulting in higher fatalities between 1945 and 1994 than any other region or sub-region. Even in Asia, while Central and South Asia produced a regional total of 2.8 million in human fatalities, East Asia's regional total is 10.4 million including the [Chinese Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Civil_War) (1 million), the Korean War (3 million), the [Vietnam War](32611.md) (2 million), and the [Pol Pot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pol_Pot) [genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cambodian_genocide) in Cambodia (1 to 2 million).

[^cite_note-Cumings_p._35-112]: &#x20;[Cumings, Bruce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bruce_Cumings) (2011). *The Korean War: A History*. [Modern Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_Library). p. 35. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-0-8129-7896-4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-0-8129-7896-4). Various encyclopedias state that the countries involved in the three-year conflict suffered a total of more than 4 million casualties, of which at least 2 million were civilians—a higher percentage than in World War II or Vietnam. A total of 36,940 Americans lost their lives in the Korean theater; of these, 33,665 were killed in action, while 3,275 died there of nonhostile causes. Some 92,134 Americans were wounded in action, and decades later, 8,176 were still reported as missing. South Korea sustained 1,312,836 casualties, including 415,004 dead. Casualties among other UN allies totaled 16,532, including 3,094 dead. Estimated North Korean casualties numbered 2 million, including about one million civilians and 520,000 soldiers. An estimated 900,000 Chinese soldiers lost their lives in combat.

[^cite_note-Lewy_pp._450-453-113]: &#x20;[Lewy, Guenter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guenter_Lewy) (1980). [*America in Vietnam*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/America_in_Vietnam). [Oxford University Press](48518.md). pp. [450](https://archive.org/details/americainvietnam00lewy/page/450)–453. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-0-19-987423-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-0-19-987423-1). The total number of Korean civilians who died in the fighting, which left almost every major city in North and South Korea in ruins, has been estimated at between 2 and 3 million. This adds up to almost 1 million military deaths and a possible 2.5 million civilians who were killed or died as a result of this extremely destructive conflict.
